The following description:

- The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.
- Implementations may implement mseccfg such that [s,u]seed is a read-only constant value 0.
- Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.

=========

RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED field to the mseccfg CSR.
RULE 2: The Zkr extension adds the USEED field to the mseccfg CSR.
RULE 3: The SSEED and USEED fields in the mseccfg CSR control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.
RULE 4: Software may discover whether access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S modes by writing 1 to the [s,u]seed fields of the mseccfg CSR and reading back the result.

The following description:

- The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.
- The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves.
- In the same passage, the source states: "Such modules can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code." The source does not explicitly identify "Such modules" as mseccfg in that sentence.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows a security boundary to be drawn around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows even if that component is not in M mode.
RULE 2: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves.

The following description:

- CSR address 0x747: Privilege MRW; Name mseccfg; Description: Machine security configuration register.
- CSR address 0x757: Privilege MRW; Name mseccfgh; Description: Upper 32 bits of mseccfg, RV32 only.

=========

RULE 5: mseccfgh, which is the upper 32 bits of mseccfg, is assigned CSR address 0x757.
RULE 6: mseccfgh, which is the upper 32 bits of mseccfg, has privilege MRW.
RULE 7: mseccfgh, which is the upper 32 bits of mseccfg, is RV32 only.

The following description:

1. mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register, formatted as shown in Figure 26, that controls security features. It exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented. Otherwise, it is reserved.
Figure 26. Machine security configuration (mseccfg) register.

2. The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.

3. When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception. When USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegalinstruction exception. If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, USEED is read-only zero.

4. When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception. When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception. If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, SSEED is read-only zero.

5. When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegalinstruction exception.

6. Table 20. Entropy Source Access Control.

| Mode | SSEED | USEED | Description |
|------|-------|-------|-------------|
| M | - | - | The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal (with a CSR read-write instruction.) Attempted read without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits. |
| U | - | 0 | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| U | - | 1 | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write. |
| S/HS | 0 | - | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| S/HS | 1 | - | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write. |
| VS/VU | 0 | - | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| VS/VU | 1 | - | A read-write seed access raises a virtual-instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal-instruction exception. |

7. The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and the MML fields in mseccfg.

8. mseccfg.RLB (Rule Locking Bypass) is a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify Locked PMP rules. When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited. When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), then mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.

9. This feature is intended to be used as a debug mechanism, or as a temporary workaround during the boot process for simplifying software, and optimizing the allocation of memory and PMP rules. Using this functionality under normal operation, after the boot process is completed, should be avoided since it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules. Vendors who don't need this functionality may hardwire this field to 0.

10. The mseccfg.MMWP (Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy) is a WARL field. This field changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule. This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset. When set it changes the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule, to denied instead of ignored.

11. The mseccfg.MML (Machine Mode Lockdown) is a WARL field. The MML bit changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit defined in Section 3.7.1.2. This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset. When mseccfg.MML is set the system's behavior changes in the following way:

- The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes: Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, it now marks a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset. The formerly reserved encoding of pmpcfg.RW=01, and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111, now encode a Shared-Region.
- An M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode. It also remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
- An S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.
- A Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits:
  - A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, so is not executable. M-mode has read/write access to that region, and S/U-mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.
  - A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/Umode, so is not writable. Both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and Mmode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
  - The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
- Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged. This restriction can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB e.g. during the boot process.
- Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.

12. If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination of pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.

13. If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field enables or disables pointer masking (see Chapter 25) for M-mode according to the values in Table 21. If Smmpm is not implemented, PMM is read-only zero. The PMM field is read-only zero for RV32.

14. Table 21. Legal values of PMM WARL field.

| Value | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| 00 | Pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0) |
| 01 | Reserved |
| 10 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64) |
| 11 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64) |

15. Smmpm implementations need to satisfy max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.

16. The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg. When MLPE field is 1, Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode. When the MLPE field is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode and the following rules apply to M-mode.

- The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.
- The LPAD instruction operates as a no-op.

17. When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg. Register mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN=64.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features.
RULE 2: mseccfg exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 3: If no extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented, mseccfg is reserved.
RULE 4: The Zkr extension adds the mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED fields to mseccfg to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.
RULE 5: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed.
RULE 7: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, seed CSR accesses from U-mode other than read-write accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.USEED is read-only zero.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed.
RULE 11: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, seed CSR accesses from S-/HS-mode other than read-write accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 12: If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.SSEED is read-only zero.
RULE 13: When the H extension is also implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, an access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction in VS or VU mode leads to a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 14: In M-mode, the seed CSR is always available as normal with a CSR read-write instruction regardless of the mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED access-control bits.
RULE 15: An attempted read of the seed CSR without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of mode and regardless of the mseccfg access-control bits.
RULE 16: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any access to the seed CSR from VS or VU mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 17: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a read-write access to the seed CSR from VS or VU mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 18: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, seed CSR access conditions from VS or VU mode other than read-write access raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 19: The Smepmp extension adds the mseccfg.RLB, mseccfg.MMWP, and mseccfg.MML fields to mseccfg.
RULE 20: mseccfg.RLB is a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify locked PMP rules.
RULE 21: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.
RULE 22: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry including disabled entries, mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.
RULE 23: The mseccfg.RLB feature is intended to be used as a debug mechanism or as a temporary workaround during the boot process to simplify software and optimize the allocation of memory and PMP rules.
RULE 24: Using mseccfg.RLB under normal operation after the boot process is completed should be avoided because it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.
RULE 25: Vendors that do not need the mseccfg.RLB functionality may hardwire mseccfg.RLB to 0.
RULE 26: mseccfg.MMWP is a WARL field.
RULE 27: mseccfg.MMWP changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule.
RULE 28: mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit that cannot be unset once set until a PMP reset.
RULE 29: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions without a matching PMP rule is denied instead of ignored.
RULE 30: mseccfg.MML is a WARL field.
RULE 31: mseccfg.MML changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit.
RULE 32: mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit that cannot be unset once set until a PMP reset.
RULE 33: When mseccfg.MML is set and pmpcfg.L is set, pmpcfg.L marks a rule as M-mode-only instead of marking the rule as locked and enforced in all modes.
RULE 34: When mseccfg.MML is set and pmpcfg.L is unset, pmpcfg.L marks a rule as S/U-mode-only.
RULE 35: When mseccfg.MML is set, the formerly reserved pmpcfg.RW=01 encoding encodes a Shared-Region.
RULE 36: When mseccfg.MML is set, the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding encodes a Shared-Region.
RULE 37: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode.
RULE 38: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only rule remains locked so that further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 39: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.
RULE 40: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits.
RULE 41: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode and is not executable.
RULE 42: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L not set, M-mode has read/write access to the region.
RULE 43: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L not set and pmpcfg.X is not set, S/U-mode has read access to the region.
RULE 44: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L not set and pmpcfg.X is set, S/U-mode has read/write access to the region.
RULE 45: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode and is not writable.
RULE 46: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region.
RULE 47: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set and pmpcfg.X is set, M-mode also has read access on the region.
RULE 48: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, the rule remains locked so that further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 49: When mseccfg.MML is set, the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode with both modes having only read-only access to the region.
RULE 50: When mseccfg.MML is set and the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding is used for a Shared-Region rule, the rule remains locked so that further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 51: When mseccfg.MML is set, adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged.
RULE 52: When mseccfg.MML is set, setting mseccfg.RLB can temporarily lift the restriction on adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region, for example during the boot process.
RULE 53: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.
RULE 54: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with Machine mode privileges from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.
RULE 55: If mseccfg.MML is not set, the pmpcfg.RW=01 combination remains reserved for future standard use.
RULE 56: When the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM is a WARL field that enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode.
RULE 57: When the Smmpm extension is implemented and mseccfg.PMM is 00, pointer masking is disabled with PMLEN = 0.
RULE 58: When the Smmpm extension is implemented and mseccfg.PMM is 01, that mseccfg.PMM value is reserved.
RULE 59: When the Smmpm extension is implemented and mseccfg.PMM is 10, pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 57, which is PMLEN = 7 on RV64.
RULE 60: When the Smmpm extension is implemented and mseccfg.PMM is 11, pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 48, which is PMLEN = 16 on RV64.
RULE 61: If Smmpm is not implemented, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.
RULE 62: mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero for RV32.
RULE 63: For Smmpm implementations, max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits needs to be satisfied, where PMLEN is selected by mseccfg.PMM, to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.
RULE 64: The Zicfilp extension adds the mseccfg.MLPE field to mseccfg.
RULE 65: When mseccfg.MLPE is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
RULE 66: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode.
RULE 67: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, in M-mode the hart does not update the ELP state and it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 68: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, in M-mode the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op.
RULE 69: When XLEN=32, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
RULE 70: mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 71: When XLEN=64, the mseccfg high-alias register mseccfgh does not exist.
======================================================= END - 4 ========================================================

The following description:

The text states that this extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR and their associated rules.

The text states that the physical memory protection rules when mseccfg.MML is set to 1 are summarized in the truth table below. In the table rows below, the condition is mseccfg.MML is set to 1, the input is the listed pmpcfg L/R/W/X bits, and the result is the listed MMode result and S/U Mode result.

| pmpcfg L | pmpcfg R | pmpcfg W | pmpcfg X | Result: MMode | Result: S/U Mode |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) |
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 1 | Access Exception | Execute-only region |
| 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode |
| 0 | 0 | 1 | 1 | Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode |
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 | Access Exception | Read-only region |
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 1 | Access Exception | Read/Execute region |
| 0 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Access Exception | Read/Write region |
| 0 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Access Exception | Read/Write/Execute region |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | 1 | Locked Execute-only region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | 0 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.* |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | 1 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 | Locked Read-only region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 | Locked Read/Execute region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Locked Read/Write region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.* | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.* |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The RISC-V extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR and their associated rules.
RULE 2: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the M-mode result is an inaccessible region with an access exception.
RULE 3: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is an inaccessible region with an access exception.
RULE 4: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=1, the M-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is an execute-only region.
RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the M-mode result is a shared data region that is read/write on M-mode and read-only on S/U-mode.
RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is a shared data region that is read/write on M-mode and read-only on S/U-mode.
RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the M-mode result is a shared data region that is read/write for both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is a shared data region that is read/write for both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=0, the M-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is a read-only region.
RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=1, the M-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is a read/execute region.
RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=0, the M-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is a read/write region.
RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the M-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is a read/write/execute region.
RULE 18: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the M-mode result is a locked inaccessible region with an access exception.
RULE 19: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is a locked inaccessible region with an access exception.
RULE 20: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=1, the M-mode result is a locked execute-only region.
RULE 21: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 22: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the M-mode result is a locked shared code region that is execute-only on both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 23: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is a locked shared code region that is execute-only on both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 24: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the M-mode result is a locked shared code region that is execute-only on S/U-mode and read/execute on M-mode.
RULE 25: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is a locked shared code region that is execute-only on S/U-mode and read/execute on M-mode.
RULE 26: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=0, the M-mode result is a locked read-only region.
RULE 27: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 28: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=1, the M-mode result is a locked read/execute region.
RULE 29: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 30: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=0, the M-mode result is a locked read/write region.
RULE 31: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=0, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 32: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the M-mode result is a locked shared data region that is read-only on both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 33: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg has L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the S/U-mode result is a locked shared data region that is read-only on both M-mode and S/U-mode.

The following description:

- "When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set and WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and it does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal-instruction exception."

- "When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit is set in hstatus , the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and the WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual-instruction exception."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set, WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, then WRS.NTO will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: If WRS.NTO is executed in VS mode, the VTW bit in hstatus is set, the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is clear, and WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, then WRS.NTO will cause a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 3: If WRS.NTO is executed in VU mode, the VTW bit in hstatus is set, the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is clear, and WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, then WRS.NTO will cause a virtual-instruction exception.

The following description:

- A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and shadowed in sstatus[10:9]. It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.

- Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.

- When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.

- Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

- If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.

- Implementations may have a writable misa.V field. Analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The vector context status field VS is added to mstatus bits [10:9].
RULE 2: The mstatus.VS field is shadowed in sstatus bits [10:9].
RULE 3: The mstatus.VS field is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field FS.
RULE 4: An attempt to execute any vector instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.
RULE 5: An attempt to access the vector CSRs raises an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.
RULE 6: When mstatus.VS is set to Initial, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
RULE 7: When mstatus.VS is set to Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
RULE 8: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.
RULE 9: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.
RULE 10: Analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.

The following description:

- When V=1, both vsstatus.VS and mstatus.VS are in effect: attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when either field is set to Off.

The following description:

- If the floating-point unit status field mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.

- If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, the vsstatus.FS field is additionally in effect for vector floating-point instructions.

- If vsstatus.FS or mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

============================================================

=========
============================================================

RULE 2: Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.
RULE 3: If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, the vsstatus.FS field is additionally in effect for vector floating-point instructions while mstatus.FS is also in effect for those instructions.
RULE 4: If vsstatus.FS or mstatus.FS is Off, then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty.

The following description:

- Global interrupt-enable bits, MIE and SIE, are provided for M-mode and S-mode respectively. These bits are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

- The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

- When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled when xIE=1 and globally disabled when xIE=0.

- Interrupts for lower-privilege modes, w < x, are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global wIE bit for the lower-privilege mode.

- Interrupts for higher-privilege modes, y > x, are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global yIE bit for the higher-privilege mode.

- If supervisor mode is not implemented, then SIE and SPIE are read-only 0.

- To support nested traps, each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts has a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes. xPIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap, and xPP holds the previous privilege mode. The xPP fields can only hold privilege modes up to x, so MPP is two bits wide and SPP is one bit wide. When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, xPIE is set to the value of xIE; xIE is set to 0; and xPP is set to y.

- An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode respectively. When executing an xRET instruction, supposing xPP holds the value y, xIE is set to xPIE; the privilege mode is changed to y; xPIE is set to 1; and xPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode (U if U-mode is implemented, else M). If y != M, xRET also sets MPRV=0.

- Setting xPP to the least-privileged supported mode on an xRET helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

- xPP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x. If privilege mode x is not implemented, then xPP must be read-only 0.

- M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to MPP then reading it back.

- If the machine provides only U and M modes, then only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in MPP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mstatus.MIE is a global interrupt-enable bit provided for M-mode.
RULE 2: mstatus.SIE is a global interrupt-enable bit provided for S-mode.
RULE 3: mstatus.MIE and mstatus.SIE are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.
RULE 4: The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus.
RULE 5: The global xIE bits in mstatus can be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction because they are located in the low-order bits of mstatus.
RULE 6: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x and mstatus.xIE=1, interrupts are globally enabled.
RULE 7: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x and mstatus.xIE=0, interrupts are globally disabled.
RULE 8: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for lower-privilege modes w < x are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global wIE bit in mstatus for lower-privilege mode w.
RULE 9: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for higher-privilege modes y > x are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global yIE bit in mstatus for higher-privilege mode y.
RULE 10: If supervisor mode is not implemented, mstatus.SIE is read-only 0.
RULE 11: If supervisor mode is not implemented, mstatus.SPIE is read-only 0.
RULE 12: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, mstatus.xPIE and mstatus.xPP form a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes.
RULE 13: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, mstatus.xPIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap.
RULE 14: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, mstatus.xPP holds the previous privilege mode.
RULE 15: For each privilege mode x, mstatus.xPP is a WARL field.
RULE 16: For each privilege mode x, mstatus.xPP can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.
RULE 17: If privilege mode x is not implemented, mstatus.xPP must be read-only 0.
RULE 18: mstatus.MPP is two bits wide.
RULE 19: mstatus.SPP is one bit wide.
RULE 20: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, mstatus.xPIE is set to the value of mstatus.xIE.
RULE 21: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, mstatus.xIE is set to 0.
RULE 22: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, mstatus.xPP is set to y.
RULE 23: When executing an xRET instruction with mstatus.xPP holding value y, mstatus.xIE is set to mstatus.xPIE.
RULE 24: When executing an xRET instruction with mstatus.xPP holding value y, the privilege mode is changed to y.
RULE 25: When executing an xRET instruction with mstatus.xPP holding value y, mstatus.xPIE is set to 1.
RULE 26: When executing an xRET instruction with mstatus.xPP holding value y, mstatus.xPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode, which is U if U-mode is implemented and otherwise M.
RULE 27: When executing an xRET instruction with mstatus.xPP holding value y and y != M, mstatus.MPRV is set to 0.
RULE 28: M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that privilege mode to mstatus.MPP and reading mstatus.MPP back.
RULE 29: If the machine provides only U and M modes, then only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in mstatus.MPP.

The following description:

- The M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension. Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1.
- When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0. For RV64, this clearing occurs regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.
- The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write. (For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit in mstatus register.)
- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit is currently 0, it is then set to 1, and the trap is delivered as expected. However, if MDT is already set to 1, then this is an unexpected trap.
- When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit. A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit. However, a trap that occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 is an unexpected trap.
- The MRET and SRET instructions, when executed in M-mode, set the MDT bit to 0.
- The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.

RULE 2: Upon reset, the MDT field that constrains the mstatus MIE bit is set to 1.
RULE 3: When an explicit CSR write sets the MDT bit to 1, the mstatus MIE bit is cleared to 0.
RULE 4: For RV64, when an explicit CSR write sets the MDT bit to 1, the mstatus MIE bit is cleared to 0 regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.
RULE 5: The mstatus MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, if the MDT bit is being set to 0 by the same write.
RULE 6: For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit is in the mstatus register.
RULE 7: When a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit that constrains the mstatus MIE bit is currently 0, that MDT bit is then set to 1, and the trap is delivered as expected.
RULE 8: When a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit that constrains the mstatus MIE bit is already set to 1, the trap is an unexpected trap.
RULE 9: When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit that constrains the mstatus MIE bit.
RULE 10: When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit that constrains the mstatus MIE bit.

RULE 12: The SRET instruction, when executed in M-mode, sets the MDT bit that constrains the mstatus MIE bit to 0.

The following description:

- For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and U-mode, respectively.

- The encoding of these fields is the same as the MXL field of misa.

- The effective XLEN in S-mode and U-mode are termed SXLEN and UXLEN, respectively.

- When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.

- When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is not supported, then SXL is read-only zero. Otherwise, it is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.

- When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is not supported, then UXL is read-only zero. Otherwise, it is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.

- If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

- Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN, and must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register. Similarly, pc bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the pc is written, it is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

- We require that operations always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.

- To reduce hardware complexity, the architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode. In practice, such settings would almost always be a software bug, but machine operation is well-defined even in this case.

- Some HINT instructions are encoded as integer computational instructions that overwrite their destination register with its current value, e.g., c.addi x8, 0.

- When such a HINT is executed with XLEN < MXLEN and bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

- This definition allows implementations to elide register write-back for some HINTs, while allowing them to execute other HINTs in the same manner as other integer computational instructions.

- The implementation choice is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: For RV64 harts, mstatus.SXL is a WARL field that controls the value of XLEN for S-mode.
RULE 2: For RV64 harts, mstatus.UXL is a WARL field that controls the value of XLEN for U-mode.
RULE 3: The encoding of mstatus.SXL and mstatus.UXL is the same as the MXL field of misa.
RULE 4: mstatus.SXL controls SXLEN, the effective XLEN in S-mode.
RULE 5: mstatus.UXL controls UXLEN, the effective XLEN in U-mode.
RULE 6: When MXLEN=32, mstatus.SXL does not exist and SXLEN is 32.
RULE 7: When MXLEN=32, mstatus.UXL does not exist and UXLEN is 32.
RULE 8: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is not supported, mstatus.SXL is read-only zero.
RULE 9: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is supported, mstatus.SXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN.
RULE 10: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is not supported, mstatus.UXL is read-only zero.
RULE 11: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is supported, mstatus.UXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN.
RULE 12: If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values of mstatus.UXL excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

The following description:

- MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.
- When MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
- When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.
- Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV.
- MPRV is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
- An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV=0.
- The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by MPP might have different XLEN settings.
- When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.
- MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.
- When MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1 in Figure 67) will succeed.
- When MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed.
- MXR has no effect when pagebased virtual memory is not in effect.
- MXR is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.
- MXR allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.
- SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.
- When SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1 in Figure 67) will fault.
- When SUM=1, these accesses are permitted.
- SUM has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
- Note that, while SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode, it is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S.
- SUM is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read-only 0.
- The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.
- In particular, they have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

Figure 67 reference: the text refers to R=1 in Figure 67 and U=1 in Figure 67. The supplied text contains an image placeholder; no further Figure 67 contents are provided.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MPRV bit of mstatus modifies the effective privilege mode, which is the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.
RULE 2: When the MPRV bit of mstatus is 0, explicit memory accesses behave normally using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
RULE 3: When the MPRV bit of mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected as though the current privilege mode were set to the privilege mode specified by the MPP field of mstatus.
RULE 4: When the MPRV bit of mstatus is 1, endianness is applied as though the current privilege mode were set to the privilege mode specified by the MPP field of mstatus.
RULE 5: The setting of the MPRV bit of mstatus does not affect instruction address-translation or instruction protection.
RULE 6: The MPRV bit of mstatus is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
RULE 7: If an MRET or SRET instruction changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M, it also sets the MPRV bit of mstatus to 0.
RULE 8: The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by the MPP field of mstatus might have different XLEN settings.
RULE 9: When the MPRV bit of mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to the XLEN specified by the MPP field of mstatus.
RULE 10: The MXR bit of mstatus modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.
RULE 11: When the MXR bit of mstatus is 0, only loads from pages marked readable with R equal to 1 will succeed.
RULE 12: When the MXR bit of mstatus is 1, loads from pages marked readable or executable with R equal to 1 or X equal to 1 will succeed.
RULE 13: The MXR bit of mstatus has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
RULE 14: The MXR bit of mstatus is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.
RULE 15: The MXR bit of mstatus allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.
RULE 16: The SUM bit of mstatus modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.
RULE 17: When the SUM bit of mstatus is 0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode with U equal to 1 will fault.
RULE 18: When the SUM bit of mstatus is 1, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode with U equal to 1 are permitted.
RULE 19: The SUM bit of mstatus has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
RULE 20: The SUM bit of mstatus is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode, but it is in effect when the MPRV bit of mstatus is 1 and the MPP field of mstatus is S.
RULE 21: The SUM bit of mstatus is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read-only 0.
RULE 22: The MXR and SUM mechanisms of mstatus only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.
RULE 23: The MXR and SUM mechanisms of mstatus have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

The following description:

- The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.
- Instruction fetches are always little-endian.
- MBE controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode (assuming mstatus.MPRV=0) are little-endian (MBE=0) or big-endian (MBE=1).
- If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0.
- Otherwise, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian (SBE=0) or big-endian (SBE=1).
- If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0.
- Otherwise, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian (UBE=0) or big-endian (UBE=1).
- For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by SBE.
- Since changing SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of these data structures, if any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
- In practice, SBE will only be changed at runtime on world switches, in which case neither the old nor new memory-management data structure will be reinterpreted in a different endianness. In this case, no additional SFENCE.VMA is necessary, beyond what would ordinarily be required for a world switch.
- If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.
- If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.
- An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if fields MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0.
- An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.
- The correspondence between addresses and byte locations is fixed and not affected by any endianness mode. Rather, the applicable endianness mode determines the order of mapping between memory bytes and a multibyte quantity (halfword, word, etc.).
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mstatus.MBE, mstatus.SBE, and mstatus.UBE are WARL fields.
RULE 2: mstatus.MBE, mstatus.SBE, and mstatus.UBE control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.
RULE 3: The mstatus.MBE, mstatus.SBE, and mstatus.UBE fields do not apply to instruction fetches, which are always little-endian.
RULE 4: When mstatus.MPRV=0 and mstatus.MBE=0, non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode are little-endian.
RULE 5: When mstatus.MPRV=0 and mstatus.MBE=1, non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode are big-endian.
RULE 6: If S-mode is not supported, mstatus.SBE is read-only 0.
RULE 7: If S-mode is supported and mstatus.SBE=0, explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian.
RULE 8: If S-mode is supported and mstatus.SBE=1, explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are big-endian.
RULE 9: If U-mode is not supported, mstatus.UBE is read-only 0.
RULE 10: If U-mode is supported and mstatus.UBE=0, explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian.
RULE 11: If U-mode is supported and mstatus.UBE=1, explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are big-endian.
RULE 12: For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by mstatus.SBE.
RULE 13: Because changing mstatus.SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of supervisor-level memory management data structures, if any supervisor-level memory management data structures remain in use across a change to mstatus.SBE, M-mode software must follow that change by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
RULE 14: In practice, mstatus.SBE will only be changed at runtime on world switches, in which case neither the old nor new memory-management data structure will be reinterpreted in a different endianness and no additional SFENCE.VMA is necessary beyond what would ordinarily be required for a world switch.
RULE 15: An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if mstatus.MBE, mstatus.SBE, and mstatus.UBE are all read-only 0.
RULE 16: An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses aside from instruction fetches if mstatus.MBE is read-only 1, mstatus.SBE is read-only 1 when S-mode is supported, and mstatus.UBE is read-only 1 when U-mode is supported.
RULE 17: The endianness mode controlled by mstatus.MBE, mstatus.SBE, and mstatus.UBE does not affect the fixed correspondence between addresses and byte locations.
RULE 18: The endianness mode controlled by mstatus.MBE, mstatus.SBE, and mstatus.UBE determines the order of mapping between memory bytes and a multibyte quantity such as a halfword or word.

The following description:

TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit in mstatus:
- Condition: none stated. Effect/description: The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations.
- Condition: TVM=1, and attempts are made to read or write the satp CSR or execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode. Effect: these attempts will raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- Condition: TVM=0. Effect: these operations (attempts to read or write the satp CSR or execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction) are permitted in S-mode.
- Condition: S-mode is not supported. Effect: TVM is read-only 0.
- Condition: none stated. Descriptive statement: The TVM mechanism improves virtualization efficiency by permitting guest operating systems to execute in S-mode, rather than classically virtualizing them in U-mode.
- Condition: none stated. Descriptive statement: This approach obviates the need to trap accesses to most S-mode CSRs.
- Condition: none stated. Descriptive statement: Trapping satp accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables.

TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus:
- Condition: none stated. Effect/description: The TW (Timeout Wait) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the WFI instruction (see Section 3.3.3).
- Condition: TW=0. Effect: the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.
- Condition: TW=1, and WFI is executed in any less-privileged mode, and it does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit. Effect: the WFI instruction causes an illegal-instruction exception.
- Condition: TW=1, and WFI is executed in modes less privileged than M, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed. Effect: an implementation may have WFI always raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- Condition: there are no modes less privileged than M. Effect: TW is read-only 0.
- Condition: none stated. Descriptive statement: Trapping the WFI instruction can trigger a world switch to another guest OS, rather than wastefully idling in the current guest.
- Condition: S-mode is implemented, and WFI is executed in U-mode, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit. Effect: executing WFI in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception, regardless of the value of the TW bit.

TSR (Trap SRET) bit in mstatus:
- Condition: none stated. Effect/description: The TSR (Trap SRET) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the supervisor exception return instruction, SRET.
- Condition: TSR=1, and attempts are made to execute SRET while executing in S-mode. Effect: these attempts will raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- Condition: TSR=0. Effect: this operation (executing SRET) is permitted in S-mode.
- Condition: S-mode is not supported. Effect: TSR is read-only 0.
- Condition: implementations that do not provide the hypervisor extension (see Chapter 22). Descriptive statement: Trapping SRET is necessary to emulate the hypervisor extension.

RULE 7: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 0, writing the satp CSR in S-mode is permitted.

RULE 9: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 0, executing a SINVAL.VMA instruction in S-mode is permitted.

The following description:

- In the mstatus register, the FS[1:0] and VS[1:0] WARL fields and the XS[1:0] read-only field are used to reduce the cost of context save and restore by setting and tracking the current state of the floating-point unit and any other usermode extensions respectively.
- The FS field encodes the status of the floating-point unit state, including the floating-point registers f0 -f31 and the CSRs fcsr , frm , and fflags .
- The VS field encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers v0 -v31 and the CSRs vcsr , vxrm , vxsat , vstart , vl , vtype , and vlenb .
- The XS field encodes the status of additional user-mode extensions and associated state.
- These fields can be checked by a context switch routine to quickly determine whether a state save or restore is required. If a save or restore is required, additional instructions and CSRs are typically required to effect and optimize the process.
- The design anticipates that most context switches will not need to save/restore state in either or both of the floating-point unit or other extensions, so provides a fast check via the SD bit.
- The FS, VS, and XS fields use the same status encoding as shown in Table 13, with the four possible status values being Off, Initial, Clean, and Dirty.

Table 13. Encoding of FS[1:0], VS[1:0], and XS[1:0] status fields

| Status | FS and VS Meaning | XS Meaning |
|--------|-------------------|------------|
| 0 | Off | All off |
| 1 | Initial | None dirty or clean, some on |
| 2 | Clean | None dirty, some clean |
| 3 | Dirty | Some dirty |

- If the F extension is implemented, the FS field shall not be read-only zero.
- If neither the F extension nor S-mode is implemented, then FS is read-only zero. If S-mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS may optionally be read-only zero.
- Implementations with S-mode but without the F extension are permitted, but not required, to make the FS field be read-only zero. Some such implementations will choose not to have the FS field be read-only zero, so as to enable emulation of the F extension for both S-mode and Umode via invisible traps into M-mode.
- If the v registers are implemented, the VS field shall not be read-only zero.
- If neither the v registers nor S-mode is implemented, then VS is read-only zero. If S-mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS may optionally be read-only zero.
- In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field is read-only zero. Every additional extension with state provides a CSR field that encodes the equivalent of the XS states. The XS field represents a summary of all extensions' status as shown in Table 13.
- The XS field effectively reports the maximum status value across all user-extension status fields, though individual extensions can use a different encoding than XS.
- The SD bit is a read-only bit that summarizes whether either the FS, VS, or XS fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory. If FS, XS, and VS are all readonly zero, then SD is also always zero.
- The SD bit is read-only and is set when either the FS, VS, or XS bits encode a Dirty state (i.e., SD=(FS==0b11 OR XS==0b11 OR VS==0b11) ). This allows privileged code to quickly determine when no additional context save is required beyond the integer register set and pc .
- When an extension's status is set to Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
- When the status is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.
- When the status is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.
- When the status is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.
- During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean.
- During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean (it should never be Dirty at restore).
- If the status is Initial, the context must be set to an initial constant value on context restore to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory. For example, the floating-point registers can all be initialized to the immediate value 0.
- The FS and XS fields are read by the privileged code before saving the context. The FS field is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context, while the XS field is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions. The status fields will also be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode.
- To improve performance, the user-mode extension can define additional instructions to allow user-mode software to return the unit to an initial state or even to turn off the unit.
- The unconfigured state would be represented as the Initial state for context save. If the same application remains running between the unconfigure and the next configure (which would set status to Dirty), there is no need to actually reinitialize the state at the unconfigure instruction, as all state is local to the user process, i.e., the Initial state may only cause the coprocessor state to be initialized to a constant value at context restore, not at every unconfigure.
- Executing a user-mode instruction to disable a unit and place it into the Off state will cause an illegal-instruction exception to be raised if any subsequent instruction tries to use the unit before it is turned back on.
- A user-mode instruction to turn a unit on must also ensure the unit's state is properly initialized, as the unit might have been used by another context meantime.
- Changing the setting of FS has no effect on the contents of the floating-point register state. In particular, setting FS=Off does not destroy the state, nor does setting FS=Initial clear the contents. Similarly, the setting of VS has no effect on the contents of the vector register state. Other extensions, however, might not preserve state when set to Off.
- Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the floating-point register state imprecisely by reporting the state to be dirty even when it has not been modified. On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate the floating-point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty. On other implementations, dirtiness might not be tracked at all, in which case the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty.
- This definition of FS does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation. Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to close a potential side channel.
- If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating-point register or the fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.
- Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean. When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.
- Table 14 shows all the possible state transitions for the FS, VS, or XS status bits. Note that the standard floating-point and vector extensions do not support user-mode unconfigure or disable/enable instructions.

Table 14. FS, VS, and XS state transitions.

| Current State Action | Off | Initial | Clean | Dirty |
|----------------------|-----|---------|-------|-------|
| At context save in privileged code: Save state? Next state | No Off | No Initial | No Clean | Yes Clean |
| At context restore in privileged code: Restore state? Next state | No Off | Yes, to initial Initial | Yes, from memory Clean | N/A N/A |
| Execute instruction to read state: Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Initial | Execute Clean | Execute Dirty |
| Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration: Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Dirty | Execute Dirty | Execute Dirty |
| Execute instruction to unconfigure unit: Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial |
| Execute instruction to disable unit: Action? Next state | Execute Off | Execute Off | Execute Off | Execute Off |
| Execute instruction to enable unit: Action? Next state | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial |

- Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits. Supervisor-level software normally uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context. Machine-level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring the extension state in their corresponding version of the context.
============================================================

RULE 64: At a context save in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, no state is saved and the next state is Off.
RULE 65: At a context save in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, no state is saved and the next state is Initial.
RULE 66: At a context save in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, no state is saved and the next state is Clean.
RULE 67: At a context save in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, state is saved and the next state is Clean.
RULE 68: At a context restore in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, no state is restored and the next state is Off.
RULE 69: At a context restore in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, state is restored to an initial value and the next state is Initial.
RULE 70: At a context restore in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, state is restored from memory and the next state is Clean.
RULE 71: At a context restore in privileged code, when mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, the restore action and next state are not applicable.
RULE 72: When an instruction executes to read state while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, an exception occurs and the next state is Off.
RULE 73: When an instruction executes to read state while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 74: When an instruction executes to read state while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Clean.
RULE 75: When an instruction executes to read state while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 76: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, an exception occurs and the next state is Off.
RULE 77: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 78: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 79: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 80: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, an exception occurs and the next state is Off.
RULE 81: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 82: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 83: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 84: When an instruction executes to disable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, the instruction executes and the next state is Off.
RULE 85: When an instruction executes to disable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Off.
RULE 86: When an instruction executes to disable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Off.
RULE 87: When an instruction executes to disable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Off.
RULE 88: When an instruction executes to enable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Off, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 89: When an instruction executes to enable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 90: When an instruction executes to enable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 91: When an instruction executes to enable a unit while mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, or mstatus.XS is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 92: The standard floating-point and vector extensions do not support user-mode unconfigure instructions for state tracked by mstatus.FS or mstatus.VS.
RULE 93: The standard floating-point and vector extensions do not support user-mode disable or enable instructions for state tracked by mstatus.FS or mstatus.VS.
RULE 94: Machine mode and Supervisor mode share a single copy of mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, and mstatus.XS.
RULE 95: Supervisor-level software normally uses mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, and mstatus.XS directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context.
RULE 96: Machine-level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring the extension state corresponding to mstatus.FS, mstatus.VS, and mstatus.XS than supervisor-level software.

The following description:

- An interrupt i will trap to M-mode (causing the privilege mode to change to M-mode) if all of the following are true: (a) either the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode; (b) bit i is set in both mip and mie; and (c) if register mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

- These conditions for an interrupt trap to occur must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt becomes, or ceases to be, pending in mip, and must also be evaluated immediately following the execution of an x RET instruction or an explicit write to a CSR on which these interrupt trap conditions expressly depend (including mip, mie, mstatus, and mideleg).
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: One required condition for interrupt i to trap to M-mode is that either the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in mstatus is set, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode.
RULE 2: The conditions for an interrupt to trap to M-mode expressly depend on the mstatus register.
RULE 3: The interrupt-trap conditions that expressly depend on mstatus must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt becomes pending in mip or ceases to be pending in mip.
RULE 4: The interrupt-trap conditions that expressly depend on mstatus must be evaluated immediately following execution of an x RET instruction.
RULE 5: The interrupt-trap conditions that expressly depend on mstatus must be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to a CSR on which the interrupt-trap conditions expressly depend, which includes mstatus.

The following description:

- For the WFI instruction: "This instruction may raise an illegal-instruction exception when TW=1 in mstatus , as described in Section 3.1.6.6."
- "The operation of WFI must be unaffected by the global interrupt bits in mstatus (MIE and SIE) and the delegation register mideleg (i.e., the hart must resume if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending, even if it has been delegated to a lessprivileged mode), but should honor the individual interrupt enables (e.g, MTIE) (i.e., implementations should avoid resuming the hart if the interrupt is pending but not individually enabled)."

RULE 3: The hart must resume from WFI if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending even if that interrupt has been delegated to a less-privileged mode, because mstatus.MIE and mstatus.SIE do not affect WFI operation.

The following description:

- The source states: "It also holds a one-bit field, MNPV, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the virtualization mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as mstatus.MPV."

- The source states: "When NMIE=0, the hart behaves as though mstatus.MPRV were clear, regardless of the current setting of mstatus.MPRV." The explicit condition is NMIE=0; the stated effect is that the hart behaves as though mstatus.MPRV were clear, regardless of the current setting of mstatus.MPRV. The source also states: "mnstatus also holds the NMIE bit."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mstatus.MPP is the field whose encoding manner is used for the privilege mode of the interrupted context in mnstatus.MNPP on entry to the RNMI trap handler.
RULE 2: mstatus.MPV is the field whose encoding manner is used for the virtualization mode of the interrupted context in mnstatus.MNPV on entry to the RNMI trap handler.
RULE 3: When the mnstatus.NMIE bit is 0, the hart behaves as though mstatus.MPRV were clear regardless of the current setting of mstatus.MPRV.

RULE 3: In a straightforward implementation, writing any field in sstatus is equivalent to writing the homonymous field in mstatus.

The following description:

- The hstatus register provides facilities analogous to the mstatus register for tracking and controlling the exception behavior of a VS-mode guest.
- The hstatus fields VTSR, VTW, and VTVM are defined analogously to the mstatus fields TSR, TW, and TVM, but affect execution only in VS-mode, and cause virtual-instruction exceptions instead of illegalinstruction exceptions.
- When VTW=1 (and assuming mstatus.TW=0), an attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual-instruction exception if the WFI does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.
- An implementation may have WFI always raise a virtual-instruction exception in VS-mode when VTW=1 (and mstatus.TW=0), even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
============================================================

RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 2: The mstatus register has fields TSR, TW, and TVM that are defined analogously to the hstatus fields VTSR, VTW, and VTVM.
RULE 3: When hstatus.VTW is 1 and mstatus.TW is 0, an attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual-instruction exception if the WFI does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.
