The following description:

- The hypervisor extension adds two fields, MPV and GVA, to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR, and modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.
- When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA not to mstatus but to mstatush. The format of mstatus is unchanged for RV32.
- The MPV bit (Machine Previous Virtualization Mode) is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode. Just as the MPP field is set to the (nominal) privilege mode at the time of the trap, the MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
- When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.
- Field GVA (Guest Virtual Address) is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode. For any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest-page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, GVA is set to 1. For any other trap into M-mode, GVA is set to 0.
- The TSR and TVM fields of mstatus affect execution only in HS-mode, not in VS-mode. The TW field affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
- Setting TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA, but has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.
- Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or for any actions taken in VS-mode.
- The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.
- When MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal.
- When MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.
- Table 49 enumerates the cases.

Table 49. Effect of MPRV on the translation and protection of explicit memory accesses.

| MPRV | MPV | MPP | Effect |
|------|-----|-----|--------|
| 0 | - | - | Normal access; current privilege mode applies. |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | U-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | HS-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | - | 3 | M-level access with no translation. |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | VU-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-level MXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | VS-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-level MXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS-level SUM bit. |

- MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions, HLV, HLVX, and HSV. The explicit loads and stores of these instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding MPRV.
- The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to the machine-level mstatus CSR or to the machine-level mstatush CSR.
RULE 2: The hypervisor extension modifies the behavior of several existing fields in mstatus.
RULE 3: When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to mstatush rather than to mstatus, and the format of mstatus is unchanged for RV32.
RULE 4: When MPV is present in mstatus, mstatus.MPV is the Machine Previous Virtualization Mode bit.
RULE 5: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is the Guest Virtual Address field.
RULE 6: When MPV is present in mstatus, the implementation writes mstatus.MPV whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 7: When a trap is taken into M-mode, mstatus.MPP is set to the nominal privilege mode at the time of the trap.
RULE 8: When a trap is taken into M-mode and MPV is present in mstatus, mstatus.MPV is set to the value of virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
RULE 9: When MRET is executed and mstatus.MPP is not 3, virtualization mode V is set to the MPV field.
RULE 10: When MRET is executed and mstatus.MPP is 3, virtualization mode V remains 0.
RULE 11: When GVA is present in mstatus, the implementation writes mstatus.GVA whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 12: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is set to 1 for a breakpoint trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 13: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is set to 1 for an address-misaligned trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 14: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is set to 1 for an access-fault trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 15: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is set to 1 for a page-fault trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 16: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is set to 1 for a guest-page-fault trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 17: When GVA is present in mstatus, mstatus.GVA is set to 0 for any trap into M-mode other than a breakpoint, address-misaligned, access-fault, page-fault, or guest-page-fault trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 18: mstatus.TSR affects execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.
RULE 19: mstatus.TVM affects execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.
RULE 20: mstatus.TW affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
RULE 21: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp.
RULE 22: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from executing HFENCE.GVMA.
RULE 23: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from executing HINVAL.GVMA.
RULE 24: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp.
RULE 25: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 has no effect on executing HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 26: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 has no effect on executing HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 27: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp.
RULE 28: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for executing HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 29: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for executing HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 30: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for any actions taken in VS-mode.
RULE 31: The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the mstatus.MPRV field.
RULE 32: When mstatus.MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal, and explicit memory accesses are normal accesses with the current privilege mode applying.
RULE 33: When mstatus.MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to mstatus.MPP.
RULE 34: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=0, and mstatus.MPP=0, the access is U-level with HS-level translation and protection only.
RULE 35: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=0, and mstatus.MPP=1, the access is HS-level with HS-level translation and protection only.
RULE 36: When mstatus.MPRV=1 and mstatus.MPP=3, the access is M-level with no translation.
RULE 37: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=0, the access is VU-level with two-stage translation and protection.
RULE 38: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=0, mstatus.MXR makes any executable page readable.
RULE 39: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=0, vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage only if they are readable at the guest-physical translation stage.
RULE 40: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, the access is VS-level with two-stage translation and protection.
RULE 41: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, mstatus.MXR makes any executable page readable.
RULE 42: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage only if they are readable at the guest-physical translation stage.
RULE 43: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, vsstatus.SUM applies instead of mstatus.SUM.
RULE 44: mstatus.MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV.
RULE 45: The explicit loads and stores of HLV, HLVX, and HSV always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding mstatus.MPRV.
RULE 46: The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register.
RULE 47: The mstatus register is not a superset of vsstatus.

The following description:

- The text defines: "An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero."

- The text states: "Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following cases:" The cases in that list that mention mstatus, each being a case for which the text says a virtual-instruction exception is raised, are:
  - "in VS-mode or VU-mode, attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0;"
  - "in VS-mode or VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0;"
  - "in VU-mode, attempts to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0, or to execute a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE);"
  - "in VU-mode, attempts to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0;"
  - "in VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR when the same access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0;"
  - "in VS-mode, attempts to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time;"

- The text states: "Because TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode), they are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode."

=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of the instruction's register operands, assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.
RULE 2: A virtual-instruction exception is raised when in VS-mode or VU-mode an attempt is made to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR and the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 3: A virtual-instruction exception is raised when in VS-mode or VU-mode, if XLEN=32, an attempt is made to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR and the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 4: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode for an attempt to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0, or for an attempt to execute a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE).
RULE 5: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode when an attempt is made to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR and the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 6: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode, if XLEN=32, when an attempt is made to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR and the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 7: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode for an attempt to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific bounded time.
RULE 8: The TSR and TVM fields in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode) and are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
======================================================= END - 45 =======================================================

The following description:

- When a trap is taken into M-mode, fields MPV and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) are set according to Table 52.
- A trap into M-mode also writes fields GVA, MPIE, and MIE in mstatus / mstatush.

Table 52. Value of mstatus / mstatush fields MPV and MPP after a trap into M-mode. Upon trap return, MPV is ignored when MPP=3.

+-----------------+-------+-------+
| Previous Mode   | MPV   | MPP   |
+-----------------+-------+-------+
| U-mode          | 0     | 0     |
| HS-mode         | 0     | 1     |
| M-mode          | 0     | 3     |
| VU-mode         | 1     | 0     |
| VS-mode         | 1     | 1     |
+-----------------+-------+-------+

- After a trap into M-mode, when Previous Mode is U-mode, MPV in mstatus / mstatush is 0 and MPP in mstatus / mstatush is 0 (Table 52).
- After a trap into M-mode, when Previous Mode is HS-mode, MPV in mstatus / mstatush is 0 and MPP in mstatus / mstatush is 1 (Table 52).
- After a trap into M-mode, when Previous Mode is M-mode, MPV in mstatus / mstatush is 0 and MPP in mstatus / mstatush is 3 (Table 52).
- After a trap into M-mode, when Previous Mode is VU-mode, MPV in mstatus / mstatush is 1 and MPP in mstatus / mstatush is 0 (Table 52).
- After a trap into M-mode, when Previous Mode is VS-mode, MPV in mstatus / mstatush is 1 and MPP in mstatus / mstatush is 1 (Table 52).
- Upon trap return, MPV is ignored when MPP=3.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When a trap is taken into M-mode, fields MPV and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) are set.
RULE 2: After a trap into M-mode, when the previous mode is U-mode, MPV in mstatus (or mstatush) is 0 and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) is 0.
RULE 3: After a trap into M-mode, when the previous mode is HS-mode, MPV in mstatus (or mstatush) is 0 and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) is 1.
RULE 4: After a trap into M-mode, when the previous mode is M-mode, MPV in mstatus (or mstatush) is 0 and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) is 3.
RULE 5: After a trap into M-mode, when the previous mode is VU-mode, MPV in mstatus (or mstatush) is 1 and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) is 0.
RULE 6: After a trap into M-mode, when the previous mode is VS-mode, MPV in mstatus (or mstatush) is 1 and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) is 1.
RULE 7: A trap into M-mode also writes fields GVA, MPIE, and MIE in mstatus (or mstatush).
RULE 8: Upon trap return, MPV in mstatus (or mstatush) is ignored when MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) is 3.

The following description:

- To store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M-mode, an MPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR.
- To store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to S/HS-mode, an SPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR. The SPELP bit in mstatus can be accessed through the sstatus CSR.
- The text states the generic x PELP rule: "When a trap is taken into privilege mode x, the x PELP is set to ELP and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED." The text provides MPELP in the mstatus CSR for M-mode and SPELP in the mstatus CSR for S/HS-mode.
- The text states: "An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode, respectively. When executing an x RET instruction, if the new privilege mode is y, then ELP is set to the value of x PELP if y LPE (see Section 23.1.1) is 1; otherwise, it is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED; x PELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED."
- The text states: "The trap handler in privilege mode x must save the x PELP bit and the x7 register before performing an indirect call/jump if xLPE=1."
- The text states: "If the privilege mode x can respond to interrupts and xLPE=1, then the trap handler should also save these values before enabling interrupts."
- The text states: "The trap handler in privilege mode x must restore the saved x PELP bit and the x7 register before executing the x RET instruction to return from a trap."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An MPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M-mode.
RULE 2: An SPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to S/HS-mode.
RULE 3: The SPELP bit in mstatus can be accessed through the sstatus CSR.
RULE 4: When a trap is taken into M-mode, MPELP in mstatus is set to ELP and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 5: When a trap is taken into S/HS-mode, SPELP in mstatus is set to ELP and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 6: When an MRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode y has yLPE equal to 1, ELP is set to the value of MPELP in mstatus.
RULE 7: When an MRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode y does not have yLPE equal to 1, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED instead of the value of MPELP in mstatus.
RULE 8: When an MRET instruction executes, MPELP in mstatus is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 9: When an SRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode y has yLPE equal to 1, ELP is set to the value of SPELP in mstatus.
RULE 10: When an SRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode y does not have yLPE equal to 1, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED instead of the value of SPELP in mstatus.
RULE 11: When an SRET instruction executes, SPELP in mstatus is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 12: If M-mode has MLPE equal to 1, a trap handler in M-mode must save the MPELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before performing an indirect call/jump.
RULE 13: If S/HS-mode has SLPE equal to 1, a trap handler in S/HS-mode must save the SPELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before performing an indirect call/jump.
RULE 14: If M-mode can respond to interrupts and MLPE is equal to 1, the M-mode trap handler should also save the MPELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before enabling interrupts.
RULE 15: If S/HS-mode can respond to interrupts and SLPE is equal to 1, the S/HS-mode trap handler should also save the SPELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before enabling interrupts.
RULE 16: A trap handler in M-mode must restore the saved MPELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before executing an MRET instruction to return from a trap.
RULE 17: A trap handler in S/HS-mode must restore the saved SPELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before executing an SRET instruction to return from a trap.

RULE 2: Unless otherwise specified, the value returned by an implicit read of the satp CSR is the same value that would have been returned by an explicit read of the satp CSR using a CSR-access instruction in a sufficient privilege mode.

The following description:

- The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations. When TVM=1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR or execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception. When TVM=0, these operations are permitted in S-mode. TVM is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

RULE 2: When the TVM bit is 1, an attempt to write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: When the TVM bit is 0, an attempt to read the satp CSR while executing in S-mode is permitted.
RULE 4: When the TVM bit is 0, an attempt to write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode is permitted.
RULE 5: Trapping satp CSR accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables.

The following description:

- The satp CSR is an SXLEN-bit read/write register, formatted as shown in Figure 63 for SXLEN=32 and Figure 64 for SXLEN=64, which controls supervisor-mode address translation and protection. This register holds the physical page number (PPN) of the root page table, i.e., its supervisor physical address divided by 4 KiB; an address space identifier (ASID), which facilitates address-translation fences on a per-address-space basis; and the MODE field, which selects the current address-translation scheme. Further details on the access to this register are described in Section 3.1.6.6.

- Figure 63. Supervisor address translation and protection (satp) register when SXLEN=32:
  | 31 | 30 | 22 21 |
  | MODE (WARL) | ASID (WARL) | PPN (WARL) |
  | 1 | 9 | 22 |

- Figure 64. Supervisor address translation and protection (satp) register when SXLEN=64, for MODE values Bare, Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57:
  | 63 | 60 59 | 44 43 |
  | MODE (WARL) | ASID (WARL) | PPN (WARL) |
  | 4 | 16 | 44 |

- Storing a PPN in satp, rather than a physical address, supports a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32.

- The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers. Some platform standards might place constraints on the values satp.PPN may assume, e.g., by requiring that all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory be representable.

- We store the ASID and the page table base address in the same CSR to allow the pair to be changed atomically on a context switch. Swapping them non-atomically could pollute the old virtual address space with new translations, or vice-versa. This approach also slightly reduces the cost of a context switch.

- Table 40 shows the encodings of the MODE field when SXLEN=32 and SXLEN=64.

- When MODE=Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses, and there is no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme described in Section 3.7.

- To select MODE=Bare, software must write zero to the remaining fields of satp (bits 30-0 when SXLEN=32, or bits 59-0 when SXLEN=64).

- Attempting to select MODE=Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining fields has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the value that the remaining fields assume and an UNSPECIFIED effect on address translation and protection behavior.

- When SXLEN=32, the satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use, whereas the encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7]!=3 are reserved for future standard use.

- When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare are reserved for future standard use.

- Version 1.11 of this standard stated that the remaining fields in satp had no effect when MODE=Bare. Making these fields reserved facilitates future definition of additional translation and protection modes, particularly in RV32, for which all patterns of the existing MODE field have already been allocated.

- When SXLEN=32, the only other valid setting for MODE is Sv32, a paged virtual-memory scheme described in Section 12.3.

- When SXLEN=64, three paged virtual-memory schemes are defined: Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57, described in Section 12.4, Section 12.5, and Section 12.6, respectively. One additional scheme, Sv64, will be defined in a later version of this specification. The remaining MODE settings are reserved for future use and may define different interpretations of the other fields in satp.

- Table 40. Encoding of satp MODE field, SXLEN=32:
  - Value 0, Name Bare: No translation or protection.
  - Value 1, Name Sv32: Page-based 32-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.3).

- Table 40. Encoding of satp MODE field, SXLEN=64:
  - Value 0, Name Bare: No translation or protection.
  - Value 1-7, Name -: Reserved for standard use.
  - Value 8, Name Sv39: Page-based 39-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.4).
  - Value 9, Name Sv48: Page-based 48-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.5).
  - Value 10, Name Sv57: Page-based 57-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.6).
  - Value 11, Name Sv64: Reserved for page-based 64-bit virtual addressing.
  - Value 12-13, Name -: Reserved for standard use.
  - Value 14-15, Name -: Designated for custom use.

- Implementations are not required to support all MODE settings, and if satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect; no fields in satp are modified.

- The number of ASID bits is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero. The number of implemented ASID bits, termed ASIDLEN, may be determined by writing one to every bit position in the ASID field, then reading back the value in satp to see which bit positions in the ASID field hold a one. The least-significant bits of ASID are implemented first: that is, if ASIDLEN > 0, ASID[ASIDLEN-1:0] is writable. The maximal value of ASIDLEN, termed ASIDMAX, is 9 for Sv32 or 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.

- The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode. Executions of the address-translation algorithm may only begin using a given value of satp when satp is active.

- Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed. The SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to the address-translation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads to those structures by a hart.

- Note that writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page-table updates and subsequent address translations, nor does it imply any invalidation of address-translation caches. If the new address space's page tables have been modified, or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction (see Section 12.2.1) after, or in some cases before, writing satp.

- Not imposing upon implementations to flush address-translation caches upon satp writes reduces the cost of context switches, provided a sufficiently large ASID space.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The satp CSR is an SXLEN-bit read/write register.
RULE 2: The satp CSR controls supervisor-mode address translation and protection.
RULE 3: The satp PPN field holds the physical page number of the root page table, which is the root page table's supervisor physical address divided by 4 KiB.
RULE 4: The satp ASID field is an address space identifier that facilitates address-translation fences on a per-address-space basis.
RULE 5: The satp MODE field selects the current address-translation scheme.
RULE 6: When SXLEN=32, satp MODE is a WARL field occupying bit 31.
RULE 7: When SXLEN=32, satp ASID is a WARL field occupying bits 30-22.
RULE 8: When SXLEN=32, satp PPN is a WARL field occupying bits 21-0.
RULE 9: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE is a WARL field occupying bits 63-60.
RULE 10: When SXLEN=64, satp ASID is a WARL field occupying bits 59-44.
RULE 11: When SXLEN=64, satp PPN is a WARL field occupying bits 43-0.
RULE 12: Storing a PPN in satp rather than a physical address supports a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32.
RULE 13: The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers.
RULE 14: Platform standards might place constraints on the values satp.PPN may assume, including requiring that all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory be representable.
RULE 15: The satp stores the ASID and the page table base address in the same CSR to allow the pair to be changed atomically on a context switch.
RULE 16: Non-atomic swapping of the satp ASID and page table base address could pollute the old virtual address space with new translations or vice versa.
RULE 17: Storing the ASID and page table base address together in satp slightly reduces the cost of a context switch.
RULE 18: When satp MODE is Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses.
RULE 19: When satp MODE is Bare, there is no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme.
RULE 20: To select satp MODE=Bare when SXLEN=32, software must write zero to satp bits 30-0.
RULE 21: To select satp MODE=Bare when SXLEN=64, software must write zero to satp bits 59-0.
RULE 22: Attempting to select satp MODE=Bare with a nonzero pattern in the satp fields other than MODE has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the values those fields assume and an UNSPECIFIED effect on address translation and protection behavior.
RULE 23: When SXLEN=32, satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use.
RULE 24: When SXLEN=32, satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7] not equal to 3 are reserved for future standard use.
RULE 25: When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare are reserved for future standard use.
RULE 26: Version 1.11 of this standard stated that the fields in satp other than MODE had no effect when MODE=Bare.
RULE 27: Making the satp fields other than MODE reserved facilitates future definition of additional translation and protection modes, particularly in RV32 where all patterns of the existing MODE field have already been allocated.
RULE 28: When SXLEN=32, the only valid satp MODE setting other than Bare is Sv32.
RULE 29: When SXLEN=32, satp MODE value 0 is Bare and means no translation or protection.
RULE 30: When SXLEN=32, satp MODE value 1 is Sv32 and selects page-based 32-bit virtual addressing.
RULE 31: When SXLEN=64, the satp MODE field defines three paged virtual-memory schemes named Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.
RULE 32: When SXLEN=64, an additional satp MODE scheme named Sv64 will be defined in a later version of this specification.
RULE 33: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE settings that are reserved for future use may define different interpretations of the other satp fields.
RULE 34: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE value 0 is Bare and means no translation or protection.
RULE 35: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE values 1 through 7 are reserved for standard use.
RULE 36: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE value 8 is Sv39 and selects page-based 39-bit virtual addressing.
RULE 37: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE value 9 is Sv48 and selects page-based 48-bit virtual addressing.
RULE 38: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE value 10 is Sv57 and selects page-based 57-bit virtual addressing.
RULE 39: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE value 11 is Sv64 and is reserved for page-based 64-bit virtual addressing.
RULE 40: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE values 12 and 13 are reserved for standard use.
RULE 41: When SXLEN=64, satp MODE values 14 and 15 are designated for custom use.
RULE 42: Implementations are not required to support all satp MODE settings.
RULE 43: If satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect and no fields in satp are modified.
RULE 44: The number of ASID bits in satp is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero.
RULE 45: The number of implemented ASID bits, termed ASIDLEN, may be determined by writing one to every bit position in the satp ASID field and then reading back the value in satp to see which ASID bit positions hold a one.
RULE 46: In satp, the least-significant ASID bits are implemented first, so if ASIDLEN is greater than zero, satp.ASID[ASIDLEN-1:0] is writable.
RULE 47: For satp, ASIDMAX, the maximal value of ASIDLEN, is 9 for Sv32 and 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.
RULE 48: The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode.
RULE 49: Executions of the address-translation algorithm may only begin using a given value of satp when satp is active.
RULE 50: Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.
RULE 51: Writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page-table updates and subsequent address translations.
RULE 52: Writing satp does not imply any invalidation of address-translation caches.
RULE 53: If the new address space's page tables have been modified or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.
RULE 54: Not imposing a requirement on implementations to flush address-translation caches upon satp writes reduces context-switch cost, provided a sufficiently large ASID space.

The following description:

- "Changing satp.MODE from Bare to other modes and vice versa also takes effect immediately, without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction."
- "Likewise, changes to satp.ASID take effect immediately."
- "Implementations must only perform implicit reads of the translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry, and must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not those that are performed speculatively."
- "This property applies even if the virtual-address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE."
- "When software recycles an ASID (i.e., reassociates it with a different page table), it should first change satp to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID, then execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID."
- "Alternatively, software can execute the same SFENCE.VMA instruction while a different ASID is loaded into satp, provided the next time satp is loaded with the recycled ASID, it is simultaneously loaded with the new page table."
- "If the implementation does not provide ASIDs, or software chooses to always use ASID 0, then after every satp write, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0. In the common case that no global translations have been modified, rs2 should be set to a register other than x0 but which contains the value zero, so that global translations are not flushed."
- "For implementations that make satp.MODE read-only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal-instruction exception."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Changing satp.MODE from Bare to a mode other than Bare takes effect immediately without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.
RULE 2: Changing satp.MODE from a mode other than Bare to Bare takes effect immediately without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.
RULE 3: Changes to satp.ASID take effect immediately.
RULE 4: Implementations must only perform implicit reads of translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or by a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry.
RULE 5: Implementations must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses to translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or by a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry when those implicit accesses are generated as a result of instruction execution and must not raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are performed speculatively.
RULE 6: The property that implementations must only perform implicit reads of translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or by a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry, and must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses generated as a result of instruction execution and not for implicit accesses performed speculatively, applies even if the virtual-address width for the translation whose data structures are accessed differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.
RULE 7: When software recycles an ASID by reassociating it with a different page table, software should first change satp to point to that different page table using the recycled ASID and then execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID.
RULE 8: When software recycles an ASID by reassociating it with a different page table, software can execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID while a different ASID is loaded into satp, provided that the next time satp is loaded with the recycled ASID, satp is simultaneously loaded with that different page table.
RULE 9: If the implementation does not provide ASIDs, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 after every write to satp.
RULE 10: If software chooses to always use ASID 0, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 after every write to satp.
RULE 11: In the common case that no global translations have been modified, when software executes the SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 after a satp write, rs2 should be set to a register other than x0 that contains the value zero so that global translations are not flushed.
RULE 12: For implementations that make satp.MODE read-only zero so that satp.MODE is always Bare, attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal-instruction exception.

The following description:

- When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of the satp register, supervisor virtual addresses are translated into supervisor physical addresses via a two-level page table. Under that condition, the 20-bit VPN is translated into a 22-bit physical page number (PPN), while the 12-bit page offset is untranslated.

- The physical page number of the root page table is stored in the satp register.

- For example, consider an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address. When the value of satp.MODE is Sv32, a 34-bit physical address is produced directly, and therefore no zero extension is needed.

- For example, consider an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address. When the value of satp.MODE is Bare, the 32-bit virtual address is translated (unmodified) into a 32-bit physical address, and then that physical address is zero-extended into a 34-bit machine-level physical address.

RULE 3: When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of the satp register, the 12-bit page offset is untranslated.
RULE 4: The physical page number of the root page table is stored in the satp register.
RULE 5: For an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address, when the value of satp.MODE is Sv32, a 34-bit physical address is produced directly and no zero extension is needed.
RULE 6: For an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address, when the value of satp.MODE is Bare, the 32-bit virtual address is translated (unmodified) into a 32-bit physical address.
RULE 7: For an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address, when the value of satp.MODE is Bare, the 32-bit physical address resulting from the 32-bit virtual address being translated (unmodified) is zero-extended into a 34-bit machine-level physical address.

The following description:

- In the virtual address translation process, let a be satp.ppn x PAGESIZE, and let i=LEVELS-1. (For Sv32, PAGESIZE=2^12 and LEVELS=2.) The satp register must be active, i.e., the effective privilege mode must be S-mode or U-mode.

RULE 3: Speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm at any time and for any virtual address is permitted as long as satp is active, meaning the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode.
RULE 4: Speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm performed while satp is active, meaning the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode, has the effect of pre-populating the address-translation cache.
RULE 5: The address-translation algorithm may only touch page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in satp.
RULE 6: The range of addresses that an implementation's page-table walker will touch is fully under supervisor control because the address-translation algorithm may only touch page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in satp.

The following description:

- The counter-enable (scounteren) CSR is a 32-bit register that controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.
- When the CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in the scounteren register is clear, attempts to read the cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
- When one of these bits is set, access to the corresponding register is permitted.
- The source names the following bit-to-register correspondence:
  - CY: cycle
  - TM: time
  - IR: instret
  - HPM n: hpmcountern
- scounteren must be implemented.
- Any of the bits may be read-only zero, indicating reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U-mode.
- Hence, they are effectively WARL fields.
- The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.
- U-mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in scounteren and mcounteren are both set.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The counter-enable scounteren CSR is a 32-bit register.
RULE 2: The scounteren CSR controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.
RULE 3: The scounteren CSR must be implemented.
RULE 4: The CY bit in scounteren corresponds to the cycle register.
RULE 5: The TM bit in scounteren corresponds to the time register.
RULE 6: The IR bit in scounteren corresponds to the instret register.
RULE 7: The HPM n bit in scounteren corresponds to the hpmcountern register.
RULE 8: When the CY bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the cycle register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: When the TM bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the time register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 10: When the IR bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the instret register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: When the HPM n bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 12: When the CY bit in scounteren is set, access to the cycle register is permitted.
RULE 13: When the TM bit in scounteren is set, access to the time register is permitted.
RULE 14: When the IR bit in scounteren is set, access to the instret register is permitted.
RULE 15: When the HPM n bit in scounteren is set, access to the hpmcountern register is permitted.
RULE 16: Any bit in scounteren may be read-only zero.
RULE 17: A read-only-zero bit in scounteren indicates that reads to the counter corresponding to that bit will cause an exception when executing in U-mode.

The following description:

- When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren.

- An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.

- A virtual-instruction exception is raised for: in VU-mode, attempts to access a non-high-half counter CSR when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.

- A virtual-instruction exception is raised for: in VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access a high-half counter CSR when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if the attempted instruction would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of the instruction's register operands assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero but is prevented from executing when V=1 because scounteren expressly disables the instruction.
RULE 2: In VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access a non-high-half counter CSR when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.
RULE 3: In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access a high-half counter CSR when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.
